T-896 completed (PR #151): the Discoveries list was rebuilt from scratch at every review, freezing the game for up to a second; it now changes only the rows a review touched, and a question about a confirmed guess is logged once, not at every count change. T-897 completed (PR #152): the question about a confirmed guess told the user to reject it "in Discoveries", a panel the app never shows; it now names "What the AI is doing" and the button. T-898 completed (PR #153): the "What the AI is doing" list has a find box and a sort choice, so a guess the person named can be found among hundreds. T-899 completed (PR #154): panels and the window could not be made smaller than the panels' added-up minimums; each panel's content now scrolls inside its box. T-900 completed (PR #155): in "What the AI is doing" the explanation and the verdict controls moved above the list, so a short panel still shows them. T-901 completed (PR #156): the user asked why the app does not chase a value like Cheat Engine; "What the AI is doing" got "I just lost a life", "I just got further" and "My score just went up" buttons that narrow the byte down across presses and take the settled byte as the person's word. T-902 completed (PR #157): a guess the user rejected stayed in the Rewards table and the Progress steps; the panel's verdicts now announce the change and a retired step counts as one, so both follow at once. T-903 completed (PR #158): the 12:31 log showed the AI's 30 ms steps stalling the GUI 40 times in every 5 s; the Learning panel now runs each step on a worker thread and the training service takes a lock so the buttons wait for the step in flight. T-904 completed (PR #159): the AI now presses "I just lost a life" for itself at every restart, voting for the byte that went down by one just before it; a byte that did so before most restarts becomes its lives guess and takes the seat; nothing is dropped and the person's confirmed guess is kept. T-905 completed (PR #160): the 13:24 log showed "I just lost a life" settling on a flickering byte and "I just got further" on the confirmed Score; a byte now has to move steadily through the window, and a byte confirmed as another kind gets no vote. T-906 completed (PR #161): the 13:24 log's "You play" stalls of 60–150 ms sat next to "picture ×28 avg 0 ms" and named nothing; every event the window handles, the panels' refreshes and the paint are now timed on the "App work" line, which leaves out work under a millisecond. T-907 completed (PR #162): T-906's profile put the discovery summary at 12 ms a call, asked for six times in every 5 s; it is now built in one pass and remembered with the knowledge's version. T-908 completed (PR #163): the user's 15:41 log had the AI dying to the same first enemy every go; the brain's 32 slots had gone to the first flags met, so the state now takes its values kind by kind (positions first, flags last), the most trusted first, and a full feature map hands an absent holder's slot to a newcomer. T-909 completed (PR #164): the same log's new line read `discoveries refresh ×14 avg 52 ms max 139 ms`; the panel now refills its events list only when the log grew and its relationships list only when the confident set differs. T-910 completed (PR #165): the same log's go-end stalls of 940 ms and 294 ms were the brain run over every remembered situation on the window's thread; the behaviour changes now read the snapshot taken as the go ended. T-911 completed (PR #166): the AI kept dying to the first enemy because a byte moving both ways was UNKNOWN and invisible to the brain; such a byte is now the position of "Something moving", named so until a button moves it, and left out of the long look. T-912 completed (PR #167): a mover in doubling bins still looked the same a jump away and touching; the state now holds each mover's distance from the player's own position, exact up close. T-913 completed (PR #168): the user's 16:47 log could not say which brain was in use or what it saw; every go now logs the brain, its exploring rate and what the state holds. T-914 completed (PR #169): the 18:01 log never listed a moving thing because a walking enemy's position changes every step and the noise rule threw it away first; a byte that moves by small hops is never noise now. T-915 completed (PR #170): the same log's distances would have been measured from the scroll position, which outranked Mario's; they are now measured from the position the stick moves or a person confirmed. T-916 completed (PR #171): the 20:49 log ran 77 goes on that build and still seated no moving thing, because a byte was labelled once at the first look and never again; the AI now rethinks any guess it made itself whenever a review's statistics say another kind. T-917 completed (PR #172): the 22:12 log showed 1,384 rethinks in an hour, most of them a byte flipping between progress and unknown; a guess is now rethought only once its byte has changed twice as often as when it was last judged. T-918 completed (PR #173): the same log's 1,384 rethinks made no moving thing because an enemy slot is filled for seconds of each go; three small hops in four now make a byte smooth and a rare byte with thirty changes over more than sixteen values is a mover. T-919 completed (PR #174): the 00:21 log still showed no moving thing after three rule changes judged on simulated bytes; every 25th review the log now prints a census of the sixteen busiest bytes with the numbers the classifier reads. T-920 completed (PR #175): the 01:52 log's censuses listed only random bytes and a frame counter; the census now also lists the smoothest bytes and the widest-ranging rare ones. T-921 completed (PR #176): the same census showed a frame counter stepping by four seated as a climbing position; a byte climbing by the same hop nearly every step is a counter now. T-922 completed (PR #177): the 11:16 census showed forty sprite positions with mover statistics still labelled "Position" because a rethink only looked at the kind; a guess is now rethought when its reasons change too. T-923 completed (PR #178): the same census's forty movers lost every position seat to plainer positions and so never got a distance seat; the distance pairs now come from everything believed, seated or not. T-924 completed (PR #179): the same census showed the frame counter still seated because the game resets it now and then; a climbing byte whose hop repeats the one before in nearly every change is a counter now, jumps or not. T-925 completed (PR #180): the Learning panel's fast-tick test failed on slow Windows runners twice; it now takes its five steps under a budget of its own. T-926 completed (PR #181): the user's 13:04 run rethought nothing because the "judged on N changes" counts saved from earlier runs could never be doubled by a run's statistics, which start from zero; the counts are forgotten when a game connects. T-927 completed (PR #182): the census line now ends with the repeated hops the counter rule reads. T-928 completed (PR #183): the user's 14:32 log rethought 460 guesses yet no moving thing was believed, because sixteen plain positions outranked every sprite for the sixteen seats of its kind; moving things now have sixteen seats of their own. T-929 completed (PR #184): the log now says what each button experiment found and which position the distances are measured from. T-930 completed (PR #185): Start pauses the user's game and the button experiment had linked it to every enemy sprite; a button that stops or replaces the whole game links to nothing now. T-931 completed (PR #186): the eight distance seats went to one moving thing measured from four copies of the player's position; own positions linked to the same buttons count once now. T-932 completed (PR #187): the user's 20:03 log had exploring at its 5% floor from go 14 while lives lost per go climbed from 0.17 to 1.83; exploring now goes back up to 25% when the last ten goes are worse than the ten before. T-933 completed (PR #188): the user's 20:03 log began five runs with a fresh brain and said nothing; every connection now logs whether the saved brain was picked up and Start from scratch asks first. T-934 completed (PR #189): the 20:03 log's survival had to be counted by hand from 160 go lines; each go line now says the lives lost and the step of the first, and every tenth go a survival line compares the last ten goes with the ten before. T-935 completed (PR #190): the 20:03 log's lives warning counted 17 checkpoint restores as the lives going up; the change statistics now skip the jump after every restore. T-936 completed (PR #191): the user typed the score 300 into Search values and was told a byte holds 0 to 255; the search now looks for any number up to 99,999,999 in every way games store it across bytes. T-937 completed (PR #192): the user's wrong reward rows looked "hard baked" because Remove only took out rewards of one's own; it now tells the AI a suggested row's guess is wrong. T-938 completed (PR #193): the user's Progress panel piled up steps from guesses the AI no longer had; a suggested step whose guess is gone is now retired. T-939 completed (PR #194): the user's 950-go log counted 45 lives lost a go from three timer-like life counts beside the confirmed one; confirming a life count, health or score now sets the AI's other guesses of that kind aside. T-940 completed (PR #195): the survival line added up every life-count guess's drops; it now counts only the best life count and best health, the person's confirmed one first. T-941 completed (PR #196): the user tracked the score's hundreds digit and it read 0 again at 1,000; a number held over several bytes is now one value in the search, the knowledge and the rewards. T-942 completed (PR #197): playing the game yourself stuttered because the AI held the emulator and released frames in bursts; while a person plays, a BizHawk link now runs at the emulator's own pace and the AI holds it again only when it watches. T-943 completed (PR #198): the user found removed reward rows "auto readded" and could not remove most of the table; a removed row now stays removed for that game, whichever guess the AI finds next, and "Bring back suggestions" undoes it. T-944 completed (PR #199): the user's recording of "Practise fast" ran at two thirds of normal speed; the log showed the window's thread busy more than half the time refreshing the knowledge panel after every step, so the Discovery and Rewards panels now refresh once every 2 s while the AI practises fast. T-945 completed (PR #200): the change tracker that watches every byte of game memory counted one byte at a time in Python; it now counts whole regions in numpy arrays, which cut that part of each step from about 5 ms to 1 ms in a synthetic profile. T-946 completed (PR #201): the relationship finder rewrote every stored cause-effect pair on every step so its rates would follow the counts; it now writes a pair at once only when its support grows and the rest at a flush every 15 steps and before a save, which cut its share of a synthetic step from 6.5 ms to 2.8 ms. One CI job failed on `test_experiments_run_on_schedule_and_on_demand` (a toy-racer value classed Flag instead of Position on ubuntu py3.11) and passed on re-run; the test passed 30 hash seeds locally on both main and the branch, so it is noted as a flake to watch. T-947 completed (PR #202): the "App work" log line now times the AI's step stage by stage (choose, emulator, observe, encode, learn) and the discovery service's share of it (track, events, long look, relationships, announce, review, experiment, save), because the user's log showed 120 ms steps with the emulator at 19 ms and nothing saying where the rest went. T-948 completed (PR #203): the first stage log showed 105 ms steps of which 56 ms were the rewards and goals services walking every relationship twice a step to see whether their suggestions should change; the knowledge now counts changes to its confident relationships in one number and the watch compares that. The same ubuntu py3.12 job tripped the 50 ms scan timing test again and passed on re-run; T-950 makes that test robust. T-949 completed (PR #204): the knowledge is saved at most once a minute while the AI plays instead of after every review and changed guess (1.1 s a save, 124 saves in 18 minutes in the user's log); a stop, a disconnect and the Save button still write at once. T-950 completed (PR #205): the big-region scan test takes the best of three runs against 50 ms or a plain Python pass over the same bytes, whichever is longer, after tripping twice on slow CI runners. T-951 completed (PR #206): the review's proposals look each candidate's bytes up in a set of the bytes the knowledge already covers instead of comparing every candidate with every relationship (2.7 s a review in the user's log), with a test proving the same candidates are skipped as before. T-952 completed (PR #207): the knowledge keeps its confident relationships in an index kept by every write, so the panel's "What leads to what" list, the suggestions and the goals read 3,000 entries instead of walking 20,000 and recomputing each confidence (136 ms a panel refresh on the GUI thread in the user's log). T-953 completed (PR #208): a guess is found by its address through a (region, offset) index the knowledge keeps, instead of a walk over every guess; the Research window asked 1,800 times at every go's end (1.4 million comparisons at the user's scale). T-954 completed (PR #209): the Research window refreshes on a finished go or a state change only while it is visible; hidden, it waits to be opened, which already refreshes it, instead of rebuilding its tables at every go's end for nobody. T-955 completed (PR #210): the log says how big a game's knowledge is when it is picked up and at every save, with the time the save took, so the next log can say whether the user's 1.1 s saves come from a huge file or a slow disk. T-956 completed (PR #211): the once-a-minute save takes its snapshot on the AI's thread (31 ms at the user's scale here) and writes the 27 MB file on a worker in pieces the interpreter can switch away from (a 4–5 ms stall instead of 1.2 s); a stop, a disconnect and the Save button wait for a running write and then write at once. T-957 found the review itself paying 34 ms a call to scan for a free variable id and 20 ms to walk every byte of every region for its statistics; a floor under the ids and one numpy pass over the seen bytes make both cheap, so a review costs what its thinking costs. T-958 found the change tracker building an object per changed byte on every step for a diff nothing on the step reads; the objects are now built the first time something asks for them. T-959 found the brain's learning step working every situation's feature row out part by part, 65 rows a step for situations the replay memory hands back over and over; the feature map now keeps a finished row per situation while its slots stay put. T-960 gave the emulator's step the two timings it lacked, the script's memory reads and its whole serve, so the next log says where the 7 ms beyond the frames go. T-961 moves the goes' start past a spot that loses a life by itself, checked holding nothing, so the next log says whether the goes still open with a loss at step 32. T-962 has the future count 0.99 a step instead of 0.95, so a life lost seconds after a mistake reaches the mistake; the next log says "The brain now looks further ahead" once. T-963 learns each step from the return over the next eight (n-step returns), so a penalty reaches its cause in a fifth of the passes. T-964 counts deaths on the value the person's own reward penalises, before the AI's guesses, and logs what is counted. T-965: T-961's probe held nothing, and a player who holds nothing is killed every 60 steps in the user's game, so the start moved 14 times in 25 minutes; the probe now holds each of the AI's buttons in turn and only a loss that comes whatever is held moves the start, at most three times from one checkpoint. T-966: the same log halved the deaths while the points per go fell from +14 to −30, nearly all from one penalty, and the survival line said nothing of it; it now gives the points per go and names the reward that moved most. T-967: that line then showed 0.0 lives lost a go under "counted on x position, Lifes": every penalised value had counted as a death and only the AI's own guesses' SETBACK events were counted; a death is now the penalised life count or health value, else the heaviest penalty, and its DOWN or SETBACK events count once per update. T-968: the 10 Nov log had the points per go climbing from +9 to +50 with the deaths flat at 1.2 a go; the log now says, with the survival line, what the AI saw a lookahead before its deaths and seldom otherwise, or that nothing stood out. T-979 (PR #225) reads the sights that stood out before the deaths in fine steps of 8, so a fall trap and the ground before it are different states. T-980 (PR #226) quiets the ERROR at the end of the user's log: the experiment harness answers "not available" when the link drops during its check. T-981 (PR #227) keeps the closer looks with the saved brain, so the user's restarts do not send the AI back to coarse steps at the spot where it kept dying. T-982 (PR #228) starts each fine situation from what the table knew of the whole bucket, so looking closer splits that knowledge instead of discarding it. T-970 to T-978 (PR #224, squash-merged 12 Nov) bring the screen recognition into the main build: the AI finds the things on the screen, follows them, works out which is itself and what the others do, and its brain is told where the nearest mover and the nearest thing that hurts are. T-983 (PR #229) holds a random move for a run of 1 to 8 steps, so the AI finds out what holding a button does; on a practice corridor it finds the held jump on ten of ten seeds against four of ten, but neither way of exploring goes on to keep what it found, which is the next learner task. T-984 (PR #230) adds Toy Hopper, a practice platform game with held jumps, walkers, coins and a dying pause, so the screen recognition and the learner can be measured here; T-985 lets the practice console run it and T-986 measures the AI on it. T-985 (PR #231) is in: `connect("fake", game="hopper")`. A first run of the whole app on it shows the user's symptoms here: the AI stalls near the start, follows itself on the screen in about a third of its looks, blames falls on touching something, and reads the scroll as its own position. T-986 merged as PR #232 (all five checks green); the bench's baseline is the measure for the tasks that follow. T-987 merged as PR #233 (all five checks green): the AI has itself, rightly, in 97% and 95% of looks on the bench. Found while measuring, for the next task: a life lost on a step without a look never reaches the watch (in one 15-go run, none of 6 did).
